# Ayn Rand

Russian-born American writer and philosopher (1905–1982)

**Alice O'Connor** (born **Alisa Zinovyevna Rosenbaum**;[\[c\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-5) February 2 \[[O.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates "Old Style and New Style dates") January 20], 1905 – March 6, 1982), better known by her pen name **Ayn Rand** ([/aɪn/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English")), was a Russian-born American writer and philosopher.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6) She is known for her fiction and for developing a philosophical system which she named *Objectivism*. Born and educated in Russia, she moved to the United States in 1926. After two early novels that were initially unsuccessful and two [Broadway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadway_theatre "Broadway theatre") plays, Rand achieved fame with her 1943 novel *[The Fountainhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead "The Fountainhead")*. In 1957, she published her best-selling work, the novel *[Atlas Shrugged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_Shrugged "Atlas Shrugged")*. Afterward, until her death in 1982, she turned to non-fiction to promote her philosophy, publishing her own [periodicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_periodicals "Objectivist periodicals") and releasing several collections of essays.

Rand advocated [reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason "Reason") and rejected [faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faith "Faith") and religion. She supported [rational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rational_egoism "Rational egoism") and [ethical egoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethical_egoism "Ethical egoism") as opposed to [altruism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altruism_\(ethics\) "Altruism (ethics)") and [hedonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedonism "Hedonism"). In politics, she condemned the [initiation of force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiation_of_force "Initiation of force") as immoral and supported [*laissez-faire* capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire_capitalism "Laissez-faire capitalism"), which she defined as the system based on recognizing [individual rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_rights "Individual rights"), including [private property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_property "Private property") rights. Although she opposed [libertarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarianism "Libertarianism"), which she viewed as [anarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarchism "Anarchism"), Rand is often associated with the modern [libertarian movement in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_movement_in_the_United_States "Libertarian movement in the United States"). In art, she promoted [romantic realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_realism "Romantic realism"). She was sharply critical of most philosophers and philosophical traditions known to her, with a few exceptions.

Rand's books have sold over 37 million copies. Her fiction received mixed reviews from literary critics, with reviews becoming more negative for her later work.[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999117%E2%80%93119-7) Although academic interest in her ideas has grown since her death,[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECocks202015-8) academic philosophers have generally ignored or rejected Rand's philosophy, arguing that she has a polemical approach and that her work lacks methodological rigor.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6) Her writings have politically influenced some [right-libertarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-libertarians "Right-libertarians") and [conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatives "Conservatives"). The [Objectivist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_movement "Objectivist movement") circulates her ideas, both to the public and in academic settings.

## Life

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Life")]

### Early life

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Early life")]

Rand was born Alisa Zinovyevna Rosenbaum on February 2, 1905, into a Jewish [bourgeois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourgeois "Bourgeois") family living in [Saint Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg "Saint Petersburg"), which was then the capital of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire "Russian Empire").[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009xiii-9) She was the eldest of three daughters of Zinovy Zakharovich Rosenbaum, a pharmacist, and Anna Borisovna ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names "Birth name") Kaplan).[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller20093%E2%80%935-10) She was 12 when the [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution "October Revolution") and the rule of the [Bolsheviks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsheviks "Bolsheviks") under [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin "Vladimir Lenin") disrupted her family's lives. Her father's pharmacy was nationalized,[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200931-11) and the family fled to [Yevpatoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevpatoria "Yevpatoria") in Crimea, which was initially under the control of the [White Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Army "White Army") during the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War "Russian Civil War").[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200935-12) After graduating high school there in June 1921,[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200936-13) she returned with her family to Petrograd (as Saint Petersburg was then named),[\[d\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-15) where they faced desperate conditions, occasionally nearly starving.[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra201386%E2%80%9387-16)

[![Book cover with black-and-white drawings and text in Russian](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Pola_Negri_by_Ayn_Rand_cover.jpg/170px-Pola_Negri_by_Ayn_Rand_cover.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pola_Negri_by_Ayn_Rand_cover.jpg)

Rand's first published work was a monograph in Russian about actress [Pola Negri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pola_Negri "Pola Negri").

After the Russian Revolution opened up Russian universities to women, Rand was among the first to enroll at Petrograd State University (now [Saint Petersburg State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg_State_University "Saint Petersburg State University")).[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200915-17) At 16, she began her studies in the department of [social pedagogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_pedagogy "Social pedagogy"), majoring in history.[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra201372-18) She was one of many bourgeois students purged from the university shortly before graduating. After complaints from a group of visiting foreign scientists, many purged students, including Rand, were reinstated.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200947-19)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200424-20) She graduated from the renamed Leningrad State University in October 1924.[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200915-17)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra19991-21) She then studied for a year at the State [Technicum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tekhnikum "Tekhnikum") for Screen Arts in Leningrad. For an assignment, Rand wrote an essay about the Polish actress [Pola Negri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pola_Negri "Pola Negri"); it became her first published work.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200949%E2%80%9350-22) She decided her professional surname for writing would be *Rand*,[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200433-23) and she adopted the first name *Ayn* (pronounced [/aɪn/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English")).[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein19999-24)[\[e\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)

In late 1925, Rand was granted a [visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_visa "Travel visa") to visit relatives in Chicago.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200918%E2%80%9319-31) She arrived in New York City on February 19, 1926.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200953-32) Intent on staying in the United States to become a screenwriter, she lived for a few months with her relatives learning English[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHicks-33) before moving to [Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles "Hollywood, Los Angeles"), California.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200957%E2%80%9360-34)

In Hollywood a chance meeting with director [Cecil B. DeMille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cecil_B._DeMille "Cecil B. DeMille") led to work as an [extra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extra_\(acting\) "Extra (acting)") in his film *[The King of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_King_of_Kings_\(1927_film\) "The King of Kings (1927 film)")* and a subsequent job as a junior screenwriter.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200434%E2%80%9336-35) While working on *The King of Kings*, she met the aspiring actor [Frank O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_O%27Connor_\(actor,_born_1897\) "Frank O'Connor (actor, born 1897)");[\[b\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-frank-3) they married on April 15, 1929. She became a [permanent American resident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_card "Green card") in July 1929 and an [American citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_nationality_law#Naturalization "United States nationality law") on March 3, 1931.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200439-36)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200971-37)[\[f\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40) She tried to bring her parents and sisters to the United States, but they could not obtain permission to emigrate.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200996%E2%80%9398-41)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200443%E2%80%9344,_52-42) Rand's father died of a heart attack in 1939; one of her sisters and their mother died during the [siege of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leningrad "Siege of Leningrad").[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopoff2024119-43)

### Early fiction

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Early fiction")]

See also: [Night of January 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_January_16th "Night of January 16th"), [We the Living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_the_Living "We the Living"), and [Anthem (novella)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthem_\(novella\) "Anthem (novella)")

[![Poster for the play Night of January 16th](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Night_of_January_16th_jury_flyer_front.jpg/170px-Night_of_January_16th_jury_flyer_front.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Night_of_January_16th_jury_flyer_front.jpg)

Rand's play *[Night of January 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_January_16th "Night of January 16th")* opened on Broadway in 1935.

Rand's first literary success was the sale of her screenplay *[Red Pawn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Pawn "Red Pawn")* to [Universal Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Pictures "Universal Pictures") in 1932, although it was never produced.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200440,_42-44)[\[g\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46) Her courtroom drama *[Night of January 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_January_16th "Night of January 16th")*, first staged in Hollywood in 1934, reopened successfully on [Broadway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broadway_theatre "Broadway theatre") in 1935. Each night, a jury was selected from members of the audience; based on its vote, one of two different endings would be performed.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200976,_92-47)[\[h\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50) Rand and O'Connor moved to New York City in December 1934 so she could handle revisions for the Broadway production.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200982-51)

Her first novel, the semi-autobiographical[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERand1995xviii-52) *[We the Living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_the_Living "We the Living")*, was published in 1936. Set in [Soviet Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Russia "Soviet Russia"), it focuses on the struggle between the individual and the state. Initial sales were slow, and the American publisher let it go out of print,[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein201013-53) although European editions continued to sell.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54) She adapted the story as [a stage play](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unconquered_\(1940_play\) "The Unconquered (1940 play)"), but the Broadway production closed in less than a week.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55)[\[i\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-57) After the success of her later novels, Rand released a revised version in 1959 that has sold over three million copies.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58)

Rand started her next major novel, *[The Fountainhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead "The Fountainhead")*, in December 1935,[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200998-59) but took a break from it in 1937 to write her novella *[Anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthem_\(novella\) "Anthem (novella)")*.[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200454%E2%80%9355-60) The novella presents a [dystopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dystopian "Dystopian") future world in which [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian "Totalitarian") collectivism has triumphed to such an extent that the word *I* has been forgotten and replaced with *we*.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200950-61)[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009102-62) Protagonists Equality 7-2521 and [Liberty 5-3000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_5-3000 "Liberty 5-3000") eventually escape the collectivistic society and rediscover the word *I*.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein201024%E2%80%9325-63) It was published in England in 1938, but Rand could not find an American publisher at that time. As with *We the Living*, Rand's later success allowed her to get a revised version published in 1946, and this sold over 3.5 million copies.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)

### *The Fountainhead* and political activism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: The Fountainhead and political activism")]

See also: [The Fountainhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead "The Fountainhead") and [The Fountainhead (film)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead_\(film\) "The Fountainhead (film)")

During the 1940s, Rand became politically active. She and her husband were full-time volunteers for Republican [Wendell Willkie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendell_Willkie "Wendell Willkie")'s 1940 presidential campaign.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200457-65) This work put her in contact with other intellectuals sympathetic to free-market capitalism. She became friends with journalist [Henry Hazlitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Hazlitt "Henry Hazlitt"), who introduced her to the [Austrian School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_School "Austrian School") economist [Ludwig von Mises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_von_Mises "Ludwig von Mises"). Despite philosophical differences with them, Rand strongly endorsed the writings of both men, and they expressed admiration for her. Mises once called her "the most courageous man in America", a compliment that particularly pleased her because he said "man" instead of "woman".[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009114-66)[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009249-67) Rand became friends with libertarian writer [Isabel Paterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabel_Paterson "Isabel Paterson"). Rand questioned her about American history and politics during their many meetings, and gave Paterson ideas for her only non-fiction book, *[The God of the Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_God_of_the_Machine "The God of the Machine")*.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200975%E2%80%9378-68)[\[j\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71)

[![Front cover of The Fountainhead](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/The_Fountain_Head_%281943_1st_ed%29_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg/170px-The_Fountain_Head_%281943_1st_ed%29_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Fountain_Head_\(1943_1st_ed\)_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg)

*[The Fountainhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead "The Fountainhead")* was Rand's first bestseller.

Rand's first major success as a writer came in 1943 with *The Fountainhead*,[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200461%E2%80%9378-72) a novel about an uncompromising architect named Howard Roark and his struggle against what Rand described as "second-handers" who attempt to live through others, placing others above themselves. Twelve publishers rejected it before [Bobbs-Merrill Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobbs-Merrill_Company "Bobbs-Merrill Company") accepted it at the insistence of editor Archibald Ogden, who threatened to quit if his employer did not publish it.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200458%E2%80%9361-73) While completing the novel, Rand was prescribed [Benzedrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benzedrine "Benzedrine"), an [amphetamine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphetamine "Amphetamine"), to fight fatigue.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200985-74) The drug helped her to work long hours to meet her deadline for delivering the novel, but afterwards she was so exhausted that her doctor ordered two weeks' rest.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200989-75) Her use of the drug for approximately three decades may have contributed to mood swings and outbursts described by some of her later associates.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009178-76)[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009304%E2%80%93305-77)

The success of *The Fountainhead* brought Rand fame and financial security.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty2007149-78) In 1943 she sold the film rights to [Warner Bros.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warner_Bros. "Warner Bros.") and returned to Hollywood to write the screenplay. Producer [Hal B. Wallis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hal_B._Wallis "Hal B. Wallis") then hired her as a screenwriter and script-doctor for screenplays including *[Love Letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_Letters_\(1945_film\) "Love Letters (1945 film)")* and *[You Came Along](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/You_Came_Along "You Came Along")*.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200468%E2%80%9371-79) Rand became involved with the [anti-Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Communist "Anti-Communist") [Motion Picture Alliance for the Preservation of American Ideals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_Picture_Alliance_for_the_Preservation_of_American_Ideals "Motion Picture Alliance for the Preservation of American Ideals") and [American Writers Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Writers_Association "American Writers Association").[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009100%E2%80%93101,_123-80) In 1947, during the [Second Red Scare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Red_Scare "Second Red Scare"), she testified as a "friendly witness" before the United States [House Un-American Activities Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Un-American_Activities_Committee "House Un-American Activities Committee") that the 1944 film *[Song of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_of_Russia "Song of Russia")* grossly misrepresented conditions in the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"), portraying life there as much better and happier than it was.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayhew2005b91%E2%80%9393,_188%E2%80%93189-81) She also wanted to criticize the lauded 1946 film *[The Best Years of Our Lives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Best_Years_of_Our_Lives "The Best Years of Our Lives")* for what she interpreted as its negative presentation of the business world but was not allowed to do so.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009125-82) When asked after the hearings about her feelings on the investigations' effectiveness, Rand described the process as "futile".[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayhew2005b83-83)

After several delays, the [film version](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead_\(film\) "The Fountainhead (film)") of *The Fountainhead* was released in 1949. Although it used Rand's screenplay with minimal alterations, she "disliked the movie from beginning to end" and complained about its editing, the acting and other elements.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200471-84)

### *Atlas Shrugged* and Objectivism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Atlas Shrugged and Objectivism")]

See also: [Atlas Shrugged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_Shrugged "Atlas Shrugged"), [Objectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivism "Objectivism"), and [Objectivist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_movement "Objectivist movement")

[![Magazine cover with a man holding lightning bolts](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Famous_fantastic_mysteries_195306.jpg/170px-Famous_fantastic_mysteries_195306.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Famous_fantastic_mysteries_195306.jpg)

Rand's novella *Anthem* was reprinted in the June 1953 issue of the pulp magazine *[Famous Fantastic Mysteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famous_Fantastic_Mysteries "Famous Fantastic Mysteries")*.[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)

Following the publication of *The Fountainhead*, Rand received many letters from readers, some of whom the book had influenced profoundly.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200991-86) In 1951, Rand moved from Los Angeles to New York City, where she gathered a group of these admirers who met at Rand's apartment on weekends to discuss philosophy. The group included future [chair of the Federal Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chair_of_the_Federal_Reserve "Chair of the Federal Reserve") [Alan Greenspan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Greenspan "Alan Greenspan"), a young psychology student named Nathan Blumenthal (later [Nathaniel Branden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Branden "Nathaniel Branden")) and his wife [Barbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Branden "Barbara Branden"), and Barbara's cousin [Leonard Peikoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_Peikoff "Leonard Peikoff"). Later, Rand began allowing them to read the manuscript drafts of her new novel, *Atlas Shrugged*.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009240%E2%80%93243-87) In 1954, her close relationship with Nathaniel Branden turned into a romantic affair. They informed both their spouses, who briefly objected, until Rand "sp\[u]n out a deductive chain from which you just couldn't escape", in Barbara Branden's words, resulting in her and O'Connor's assent.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009256%E2%80%93259-88) Historian [Jennifer Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_Burns_\(historian\) "Jennifer Burns (historian)") concludes that O'Connor was likely "the hardest hit" emotionally by the affair.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009157-89)

Published in 1957, *Atlas Shrugged* is considered Rand's *[magnum opus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnum_opus "Magnum opus")*.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013106-90)[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayhew2005b78-91) She described the novel's theme as "the role of the mind in man's existence—and, as a corollary, the demonstration of a new moral philosophy: the morality of rational self-interest".[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92) It advocates the core tenets of Rand's philosophy of [Objectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivism "Objectivism") and expresses her concept of human achievement. The plot involves a [dystopian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dystopia "Dystopia") United States in which the most creative industrialists, scientists, and artists respond to a [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state "Welfare state") government by going on [strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strike_action "Strike action") and retreating to a hidden valley where they build an independent free economy. The novel's hero and leader of the strike, [John Galt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Galt "John Galt"), describes it as stopping "the motor of the world" by withdrawing the minds of individuals contributing most to the nation's wealth and achievements.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199954-93) The novel contains an exposition of Objectivism in a lengthy monologue delivered by Galt.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94)

Despite many negative reviews, *Atlas Shrugged* became an international bestseller,[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns20092-95) but the reaction of intellectuals to the novel discouraged and depressed Rand.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009178-76)[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009303%E2%80%93306-96) *Atlas Shrugged* was her last completed work of fiction, marking the end of her career as a novelist and the beginning of her role as a popular philosopher.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYounkins20071-97)

In 1958, Nathaniel Branden established the Nathaniel Branden Lectures, later incorporated as the [Nathaniel Branden Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Branden_Institute "Nathaniel Branden Institute") (NBI), to promote Rand's philosophy through public lectures. He and Rand co-founded *[The Objectivist Newsletter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Objectivist_Newsletter "The Objectivist Newsletter")* (later renamed *The Objectivist*) in 1962 to circulate articles about her ideas;[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009321-98) she later republished some of these articles in book form. Rand was unimpressed by many of the NBI students[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009303-99) and held them to strict standards, sometimes reacting coldly or angrily to those who disagreed with her.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty2007237%E2%80%93238-100)[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009329-101)[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009235-102) Critics, including some former NBI students and Branden himself, later said the NBI culture was one of intellectual conformity and excessive reverence for Rand. Some described the NBI or the [Objectivist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_movement "Objectivist movement") as a [cult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult "Cult") or religion.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010105%E2%80%93106-103)[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009232%E2%80%93233-104) Rand expressed opinions on a wide range of topics, from literature and music to sexuality and facial hair. Some of her followers mimicked her preferences, wearing clothes to match characters from her novels and buying furniture like hers.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009236%E2%80%93237-105) Some former NBI students believed the extent of these behaviors was exaggerated, and the problem was concentrated among Rand's closest followers in New York.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009235-102)[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty2007235-106)

### Later years

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Later years")]

Throughout the 1960s and 70s, Rand developed and promoted her Objectivist philosophy through nonfiction and speeches,[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranden1986315%E2%80%93316-107)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199914-108) including annual lectures at the [Ford Hall Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_Hall_Forum "Ford Hall Forum").[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199916-109) In answers to audience questions, she took controversial stances on political and social issues. These included supporting abortion rights,[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009320%E2%80%93321-110) opposing the [Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vietnam_War "Vietnam War") and the [military draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_draft "Military draft") (but condemning many [draft dodgers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_dodgers "Draft dodgers") as "bums"),[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009228%E2%80%93229,_265-111)[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009352-112) supporting Israel in the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War "Yom Kippur War") of 1973 against a coalition of Arab nations as "civilized men fighting savages",[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler2021202_n114-113)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009266-114) claiming [European colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas "European colonization of the Americas") had the right to invade and take land inhabited by [American Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Indigenous peoples of the Americas"),[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009266-114)[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115) and calling homosexuality "immoral" and "disgusting", despite advocating the repeal of all laws concerning it.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009362,_519-116) She endorsed several [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_\(United_States\) "Republican Party (United States)") candidates for president of the United States, most strongly [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater "Barry Goldwater") in [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_United_States_presidential_election "1964 United States presidential election").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009204%E2%80%93206-117)[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009322%E2%80%93323-118)

[![Twin gravestone for Frank O'Connor and Ayn Rand O'Connor](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Ayn_Rand_Marker.jpg/220px-Ayn_Rand_Marker.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ayn_Rand_Marker.jpg)

Grave marker for Rand and her husband at [Kensico Cemetery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kensico_Cemetery "Kensico Cemetery") in [Valhalla, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valhalla,_New_York "Valhalla, New York")[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009405-119)

In 1964, Nathaniel Branden began an affair with the young actress [Patrecia Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrecia_Scott "Patrecia Scott"), whom he later married. Nathaniel and Barbara Branden kept the affair hidden from Rand. As her relationship with Nathaniel Branden deteriorated, Rand had her husband be present for difficult conversations between her and Branden.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009360%E2%80%93361-120) In 1968, Rand learned about Branden's relationship with Scott. Though her romantic involvement with Nathaniel Branden was already over,[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting2004101-121) Rand ended her relationship with both Brandens, and the NBI closed.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009374%E2%80%93375-122) She published an article in *The Objectivist* repudiating Nathaniel Branden for dishonesty and "irrational behavior in his private life".[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009378%E2%80%93379-123) In subsequent years, Rand and several more of her closest associates parted company.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009276-124)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009398%E2%80%93400-125)

Rand's younger sister Eleonora Drobisheva (née *Rosenbaum*, 1910–1999) visited her in the US in 1973 at the former's invitation, but did not accept her lifestyle and views, as well as finding little literary merit in her works. She subsequently returned to the Soviet Union and spent the rest of her life in Leningrad (later [Saint Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg "Saint Petersburg")).[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-126)

Rand had surgery for lung cancer in 1974 after decades of heavy smoking.[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009391%E2%80%93393-127) In 1976, she retired from her newsletter and, despite her lifelong objections to any government-run program, was enrolled in and subsequently claimed [Social Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Security_\(United_States\) "Social Security (United States)") and [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\) "Medicare (United States)") with the aid of a social worker.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcConnell2010520%E2%80%93521-128)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiss201262-129) Her activities in the Objectivist movement declined, especially after her husband died on November 9, 1979.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranden1986392%E2%80%93395-130) One of her final projects was a never-completed television adaptation of *Atlas Shrugged*.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009406-131)

On March 6, 1982, Rand died of heart failure at her home in New York City.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009410-132) Her funeral included a 6-foot (1.8 m) floral arrangement in the shape of a dollar sign.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein201020-133) In her will, Rand named Peikoff as her heir.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009400-134)

## Literary approach, influences and reception

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Literary approach, influences and reception")]

Rand described her approach to literature as "[romantic realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_realism "Romantic realism")".[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009179-135) She wanted her fiction to present the world "as it could be and should be", rather than as it was.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136) This approach led her to create highly stylized situations and characters. Her fiction typically has [protagonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protagonists "Protagonists") who are heroic individualists, depicted as fit and attractive.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199926-137) Her villains support duty and collectivist moral ideals. Rand often describes them as unattractive, and some have names that suggest negative traits, such as Wesley Mouch in *Atlas Shrugged*.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199927-138)[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker198799%E2%80%93105-139)

Rand considered plot a critical element of literature,[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETorresKamhi200064-140) and her stories typically have what biographer Anne Heller described as "tight, elaborate, fast-paced plotting".[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200964-141) [Romantic triangles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_triangle "Romantic triangle") are a common plot element in Rand's fiction; in most of her novels and plays, the main female character is romantically involved with at least two men.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan201944-142)[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-143)

### Influences

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Influences")]

[![Photo of Victor Hugo](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Victor_Hugo_by_%C3%89tienne_Carjat_1876_-_full.jpg/170px-Victor_Hugo_by_%C3%89tienne_Carjat_1876_-_full.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Victor_Hugo_by_%C3%89tienne_Carjat_1876_-_full.jpg)

Rand admired the novels of [Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo "Victor Hugo").

In school, Rand read works by [Fyodor Dostoevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoevsky "Fyodor Dostoevsky"), [Victor Hugo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hugo "Victor Hugo"), [Edmond Rostand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmond_Rostand "Edmond Rostand"), and [Friedrich Schiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Schiller "Friedrich Schiller"), who became her favorites.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritting200417,_22-144) She considered them to be among the "top rank" of [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism "Romanticism") writers because of their focus on moral themes and their skill at constructing plots.[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETorresKamhi200059-145) Hugo was an important influence on her writing, especially her approach to plotting. In the introduction she wrote for an English-language edition of his novel *[Ninety-Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-Three "Ninety-Three")*, Rand called him "the greatest novelist in world literature".[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200932%E2%80%9333-146)

Although Rand disliked most Russian literature, her depictions of her heroes show the influence of the [Russian Symbolists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Symbolists "Russian Symbolists")[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrigorovskaya2018315%E2%80%93325-147) and other nineteenth-century Russian writing, most notably the 1863 novel *[What Is to Be Done?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/What_Is_to_Be_Done%3F_\(novel\) "What Is to Be Done? (novel)")* by [Nikolay Chernyshevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Chernyshevsky "Nikolay Chernyshevsky").[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKizilov2021106-148)[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiner20206%E2%80%937-149) Scholars of Russian literature see in Chernyshevsky's character Rakhmetov, an "ascetic revolutionist", the template for Rand's literary heroes and heroines.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson200047%E2%80%9367-150)

Rand's experience of the Russian Revolution and early Communist Russia influenced the portrayal of her villains. Beyond *We the Living*, which is set in Russia, this influence can be seen in the ideas and rhetoric of Ellsworth Toohey in *The Fountainhead*,[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERosenthal2004220%E2%80%93223-151) and in the destruction of the economy in *Atlas Shrugged*.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKizilov2021109-152)[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERosenthal2004200%E2%80%93206-153)

Rand's descriptive style echoes her early career writing scenarios and scripts for movies; her novels have many narrative descriptions that resemble early Hollywood movie scenarios. They often follow common film editing conventions, such as having a broad [establishing shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Establishing_shot "Establishing shot") description of a scene followed by [close-up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Close-up "Close-up") details, and her descriptions of women characters often take a "[male gaze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Male_gaze "Male gaze")" perspective.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-154)

### Contemporary reviews

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Contemporary reviews")]

[![Photo of Rand](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/Ayn_Rand_%281957_Phyllis_Cerf_portrait%29.jpg/170px-Ayn_Rand_%281957_Phyllis_Cerf_portrait%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ayn_Rand_\(1957_Phyllis_Cerf_portrait\).jpg)

Rand in 1957

The first reviews Rand received were for *Night of January 16th*. Reviews of the Broadway production were largely positive, but Rand considered even positive reviews to be embarrassing because of significant changes made to her script by the producer.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranden1986122%E2%80%93124-155) Although Rand believed that *We the Living* was not widely reviewed, over 200 publications published approximately 125 different reviews. Overall, they were more positive than those she received for her later work.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-156) *Anthem* received little review attention, both for its first publication in England and for subsequent re-issues.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-157)

Rand's first bestseller, *The Fountainhead*, received far fewer reviews than *We the Living*, and reviewers' opinions were mixed.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tfreviews-158) [Lorine Pruette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorine_Pruette "Lorine Pruette")'s positive review in *[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")*, which called the author "a writer of great power" who wrote "brilliantly, beautifully and bitterly",[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPruette1943BR7-159) was one that Rand greatly appreciated.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009152-160) There were other positive reviews, but Rand dismissed most of them for either misunderstanding her message or for being in unimportant publications.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tfreviews-158) Some negative reviews said the novel was too long;[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999117%E2%80%93119-7) others called the characters unsympathetic and Rand's style "offensively pedestrian".[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-tfreviews-158)

*Atlas Shrugged* was widely reviewed, and many of the reviews were strongly negative.[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999117%E2%80%93119-7)[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-asreviews-161) *Atlas Shrugged* received positive reviews from a few publications,[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-asreviews-161) but Rand scholar [Mimi Reisel Gladstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimi_Reisel_Gladstein "Mimi Reisel Gladstein") later wrote that "reviewers seemed to vie with each other in a contest to devise the cleverest put-downs", with reviews including comments that it was "written out of hate" and showed "remorseless hectoring and prolixity".[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999117%E2%80%93119-7) [Whittaker Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whittaker_Chambers "Whittaker Chambers") wrote what was later called the novel's most "notorious" review[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009174-162)[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty2007659_n4-163) for the conservative magazine *[National Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Review "National Review")*. He accused Rand of supporting a godless system (which he related to that of the [Soviets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_the_Soviet_Union "Religion in the Soviet Union")), claiming, "From almost any page of *Atlas Shrugged*, a voice can be heard ... commanding: 'To a gas chamber—go!'".[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChambers1957596-164)[\[k\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-167)

Rand's nonfiction received far fewer reviews than her novels. The tenor of the criticism for her first nonfiction book, *[For the New Intellectual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_the_New_Intellectual "For the New Intellectual")*, was similar to that for *Atlas Shrugged*.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999119-168) Philosopher [Sidney Hook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sidney_Hook "Sidney Hook") likened her certainty to "the way philosophy is written in the Soviet Union",[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHook196128-169) and author [Gore Vidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gore_Vidal "Gore Vidal") called her viewpoint "nearly perfect in its immorality".[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVidal1962\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsrockingboat00vidapage234_234]-170) These reviews set the pattern for reaction to her ideas among [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States "Modern liberalism in the United States") critics.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009193%E2%80%93194-171) Her subsequent books got progressively less review attention.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999119-168)

### Academic assessments of Rand's fiction

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Academic assessments of Rand's fiction")]

Academic consideration of Rand as a literary figure during her life was limited. Mimi Reisel Gladstein could not find any scholarly articles about Rand's novels when she began researching her in 1973, and only three such articles appeared during the rest of the 1970s.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2003373%E2%80%93374,_379%E2%80%93381-172) Since her death, scholars of English and American literature have continued largely to ignore her work,[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2003375-173) although attention to her literary work has increased since the 1990s.[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2003384%E2%80%93391-174) Several academic book series about important authors cover Rand and her works,[\[l\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-176) as do popular study guides like [CliffsNotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CliffsNotes "CliffsNotes") and [SparkNotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SparkNotes "SparkNotes").[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2003382%E2%80%93389-177) In *[The Literary Encyclopedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Literary_Encyclopedia "The Literary Encyclopedia")* entry for Rand written in 2001, [John David Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_David_Lewis "John David Lewis") declared that "Rand wrote the most intellectually challenging fiction of her generation."[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELewis2001-178) In 2019, [Lisa Duggan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Duggan "Lisa Duggan") described Rand's fiction as popular and influential on many readers, despite being easy to criticize for "her cartoonish characters and melodramatic plots, her rigid moralizing, her middle- to lowbrow aesthetic preferences ... and philosophical strivings".[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan20194-179)

## Philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Philosophy")]

| [Objectivist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_movement "Objectivist movement")                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
