                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

Main article: [Objectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivism "Objectivism")

Rand called her philosophy "Objectivism", describing its essence as "the concept of man as a heroic being, with his own happiness as the moral purpose of his life, with productive achievement as his noblest activity, and reason as his only absolute".[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERand19921170%E2%80%931171-180) She considered Objectivism a [systematic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systematic_philosophy "Systematic philosophy") and laid out positions on [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics "Metaphysics"), [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology "Epistemology"), [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics "Ethics"), [aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesthetics "Aesthetics"), and [political philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_philosophy "Political philosophy").[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladsteinSciabarra19992-181)

### Metaphysics and epistemology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Metaphysics and epistemology")]

In metaphysics, Rand supported [philosophical realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_realism "Philosophical realism") and opposed anything she regarded as mysticism or supernaturalism, including all forms of religion.[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-182) Rand believed in [free will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_will "Free will") as a form of [agent causation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agent_causation "Agent causation") and rejected [determinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Determinism "Determinism").[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-183)

Rand also related her aesthetics to metaphysics by defining art as a "selective re-creation of reality according to an artist's metaphysical value-judgments".[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETorresKamhi200026-184) According to her, art allows philosophical concepts to be presented in a concrete form that can be grasped easily, thereby fulfilling a need of human consciousness.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013191%E2%80%93192-185) As a writer, the art form Rand focused on most closely was literature. In works such as *[The Romantic Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Romantic_Manifesto "The Romantic Manifesto")* and *[The Art of Fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Art_of_Fiction:_A_Guide_for_Writers_and_Readers "The Art of Fiction: A Guide for Writers and Readers")*, she described [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism "Romanticism") as the approach that most accurately reflects the existence of human free will.[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGotthelf200093-186)

In epistemology, Rand considered all knowledge to be based on forming higher levels of understanding from sense perception, the validity of which she considered [axiomatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axiom "Axiom").[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGotthelf200054-187) She described reason as "the faculty that identifies and integrates the material provided by man's senses".[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-188) Rand rejected all claims of non-perceptual knowledge, including "'instinct,' 'intuition,' 'revelation,' or any form of 'just knowing'".[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013403_n20-189) In her *[Introduction to Objectivist Epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduction_to_Objectivist_Epistemology "Introduction to Objectivist Epistemology")*, Rand presented a theory of concept formation and rejected the [analytic–synthetic dichotomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytic%E2%80%93synthetic_dichotomy "Analytic–synthetic dichotomy").[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalmieriGotthelf20051997-190)[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199985%E2%80%9386-191) She believed epistemology was a foundational branch of philosophy and considered the advocacy of reason to be the single most significant aspect of her philosophy.[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-192)

Commentators, including [Hazel Barnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazel_Barnes "Hazel Barnes"), Nathaniel Branden, and [Albert Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Ellis "Albert Ellis"), have criticized Rand's focus on the importance of reason. Barnes and Ellis said Rand was too dismissive of emotion and failed to recognize its importance in human life. Branden said Rand's emphasis on reason led her to denigrate emotions and create unrealistic expectations of how consistently rational human beings should be.[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013173%E2%80%93176-193)

### Ethics and politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Ethics and politics")]

In ethics, Rand argued for [rational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rational_egoism "Rational egoism") and [ethical egoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethical_egoism "Ethical egoism") (rational self-interest), as the guiding moral principle. She said the individual should "exist for his own sake, neither sacrificing himself to others nor sacrificing others to himself".[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-194) Rand referred to egoism as "the virtue of selfishness" in her [book of that title](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Virtue_of_Selfishness "The Virtue of Selfishness").[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukathas199855-195) In it, she presented her solution to the [is–ought problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Is%E2%80%93ought_problem "Is–ought problem") by describing a [meta-ethical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meta-ethical "Meta-ethical") theory that based morality in the needs of "man's survival *qua* man", which requires the use of a rational mind.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6) She condemned ethical altruism as incompatible with the requirements of human life and happiness,[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6) and held the [initiation of force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiation_of_force "Initiation of force") was evil and irrational,[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGotthelf200091-196) writing in *Atlas Shrugged* that "Force and mind are opposites".[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013252-197)

Rand's ethics and politics are the most criticized areas of her philosophy.[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDen_UylRasmussen1986165-198) Several authors, including [Robert Nozick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Nozick "Robert Nozick") and William F. O'Neill in two of the earliest academic critiques of her ideas,[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999100,_115-199) said she failed in her attempt to solve the is–ought problem.[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013224-200) Critics have called her definitions of *egoism* and *altruism* biased and inconsistent with normal usage.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013220-201) Critics from religious traditions oppose her [atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism "Atheism") and her rejection of altruism.[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaker1987140%E2%80%93142-202)

Rand's political philosophy emphasized [individual rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_rights "Individual rights"), including [property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_property "Private property"). She considered *[laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire "Laissez-faire")* [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism "Capitalism") the only moral social system because in her view it was the only system based on protecting those rights.[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGotthelf200091%E2%80%9392-203) Rand opposed [collectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivism_and_individualism "Collectivism and individualism") and [statism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statism "Statism"),[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-204) which she considered to include many specific forms of government, such as [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism"), [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism "Fascism"), [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism"), [theocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theocracy "Theocracy"), and the [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state "Welfare state").[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-205) Her preferred form of government was a [constitutional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional "Constitutional") republic that is limited to the protection of individual rights.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeikoff1991367%E2%80%93368-206) Although her political views are often classified as [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") or [libertarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian "Libertarian"), Rand preferred the term "radical for capitalism". She worked with conservatives on political projects but disagreed with them over issues such as religion and ethics.[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009174%E2%80%93177,_209,_230%E2%80%93231-207)[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty2007189%E2%80%93190-208) Rand rejected [anarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarchism "Anarchism") as a naive theory based in [subjectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subjectivism "Subjectivism") that would lead to collectivism in practice,[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013261%E2%80%93262-209) and denounced libertarianism, which she associated with anarchism.[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013248%E2%80%93249-210)[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009268%E2%80%93269-211)

Several critics, including Nozick, have said her attempt to justify individual rights based on egoism fails.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-212) Others, like libertarian philosopher [Michael Huemer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Huemer "Michael Huemer"), have gone further, saying that her support of egoism and her support of individual rights are inconsistent positions.[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-213) Some critics, like [Roy Childs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Childs "Roy Childs"), have said that her opposition to the initiation of force should lead to support of anarchism, rather than limited government.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013260,_442_n33-214)[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999116-215)

### Relationship to other philosophers

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Relationship to other philosophers")]

[![Marble statue of Aristotle](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg/129px-Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle_Altemps_Inv8575.jpg)

[![Painting of Immanuel Kant](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg/155px-Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Immanuel_Kant_portrait_c1790.jpg)

Rand claimed [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle "Aristotle") (left) as her primary philosophical influence, and strongly criticized [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant") (right).

Except for Aristotle, [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas "Thomas Aquinas") and [classical liberals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberals "Classical liberals"), Rand was sharply critical[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013111-216) of most philosophers and philosophical traditions known to her.[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Neill197718%E2%80%9320-217)[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra201311-218) Acknowledging Aristotle as her greatest influence,[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns20092-95) Rand remarked that in the [history of philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_philosophy "History of philosophy") she could only recommend "three A's"—Aristotle, Aquinas, and Ayn Rand.[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra201311-218) In a 1959 interview with [Mike Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Wallace "Mike Wallace"), when asked where her philosophy came from, she responded: "Out of my own mind, with the sole acknowledgement of a debt to Aristotle, the only philosopher who ever influenced me."[\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPodritskeSchwartz2009174%E2%80%93175-219)

In an article for the *[Claremont Review of Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claremont_Review_of_Books "Claremont Review of Books")*, political scientist [Charles Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Murray_\(political_scientist\) "Charles Murray (political scientist)") criticized Rand's claim that her only "philosophical debt" was to Aristotle. He asserted her ideas were derivative of previous thinkers such as [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke "John Locke") and [Friedrich Nietzsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Nietzsche "Friedrich Nietzsche").[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurray2010-220) Rand took early inspiration from Nietzsche,[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200916,_22-221)[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra201394%E2%80%9399-222) and scholars have found indications of this in Rand's private journals. In 1928, she alluded to his idea of the "[superman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Cbermensch "Übermensch")" in notes for an unwritten novel whose protagonist was inspired by the murderer [William Edward Hickman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Edward_Hickman "William Edward Hickman").[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200924%E2%80%9325-223) There are other indications of Nietzsche's influence in passages from the first edition of *We the Living* (which Rand later revised),[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-224) and in her overall writing style.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6)[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-225) By the time she wrote *The Fountainhead*, Rand had turned against Nietzsche's ideas,[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200942-226)[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200941,_68-227) and the extent of his influence on her even during her early years is disputed.[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009303%E2%80%93304-228) Rand's views also may have been influenced by the promotion of egoism among the [Russian nihilists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_nihilists "Russian nihilists"), including Chernyshevsky and [Dmitry Pisarev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Pisarev "Dmitry Pisarev"),[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeinacht202131%E2%80%9332-229)[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOfford202240-230) although there is no direct evidence that she read them.[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeinacht202112%E2%80%9313-231)[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOfford202238%E2%80%9339-232)

Rand considered [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant") her philosophical opposite and "the most evil man in mankind's history";[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERand19714-233) she believed his epistemology undermined reason and his ethics opposed self-interest.[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-234) Philosophers George Walsh and Fred Seddon have argued she misinterpreted Kant and exaggerated their differences.[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHill2001195-235)[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERegister2004155-236) She was also critical of [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato") and viewed his differences with Aristotle on questions of metaphysics and epistemology as the primary conflict in the history of philosophy.[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-237)

Rand's relationship with contemporary philosophers was mostly antagonistic. She was not an academic and did not participate in academic discourse.[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMachan2000121-238)[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler202124%E2%80%9326-239) She was dismissive of critics and wrote about ideas she disagreed with in a polemical manner without in-depth analysis.[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler202124%E2%80%9326-239)[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMachan2000147-240) Academic philosophers in turn viewed her negatively and dismissed her as an unimportant figure who should not be considered a philosopher or given any serious response.[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6)[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler202127-241)[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECleary2018-242)

### Early academic reaction

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Early academic reaction")]

During Rand's lifetime, her work received little attention from academic scholars.[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra20131%E2%80%932-243) In 1967, [John Hospers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hospers "John Hospers") discussed Rand's ethical ideas in the second edition of his textbook, *An Introduction to Philosophical Analysis*. That same year, Hazel Barnes included a chapter critiquing Objectivism in her book *An Existentialist Ethics*.[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009188,_325-244) When the first full-length academic book about Rand's philosophy appeared in 1971, its author declared writing about Rand "a treacherous undertaking" that could lead to "guilt by association" for taking her seriously.[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Neill19773-245) A few articles about Rand's ideas appeared in academic journals before her death in 1982, many of them in *[The Personalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Personalist "The Personalist")*.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999115-246) One of these was "On the Randian Argument" by libertarian philosopher Robert Nozick, who criticized her meta-ethical arguments.[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013224-200) In the same journal, other philosophers argued that Nozick misstated Rand's case.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999115-246) In an article responding to Nozick, [Douglas Den Uyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Den_Uyl "Douglas Den Uyl") and [Douglas B. Rasmussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_B._Rasmussen "Douglas B. Rasmussen") defended her positions, but described her style as "literary, hyperbolic and emotional".[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDen_UylRasmussen1978203-247)

After her death, interest in Rand's ideas increased gradually.[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010114%E2%80%93122-248)[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESalmieriGotthelf20051995-249) *[The Philosophic Thought of Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Philosophic_Thought_of_Ayn_Rand "The Philosophic Thought of Ayn Rand")*, a 1984 collection of essays about Objectivism edited by Den Uyl and Rasmussen, was the first academic book about Rand's ideas published after her death.[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999101-250) In one essay, political writer Jack Wheeler wrote that despite "the incessant bombast and continuous venting of Randian rage", Rand's ethics are "a most immense achievement, the study of which is vastly more fruitful than any other in contemporary thought".[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-251) In 1987, the Ayn Rand Society was founded as an affiliate of the [American Philosophical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Philosophical_Association "American Philosophical Association").[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGotthelf20002,_25-252)

In a 1995 entry about Rand in *Contemporary Women Philosophers*, Jenny A. Heyl described a divergence in how different academic specialties viewed Rand. She said that Rand's philosophy "is regularly omitted from academic philosophy. Yet, throughout literary academia, Ayn Rand is considered a philosopher."[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeyl1995223-253) Writing in the 1998 edition of the *[Routledge Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Routledge_Encyclopedia_of_Philosophy "Routledge Encyclopedia of Philosophy")*, political theorist [Chandran Kukathas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandran_Kukathas "Chandran Kukathas") summarized the mainstream philosophical reception of her work in two parts. He said most commentators view her ethical argument as an unconvincing variant of Aristotle's ethics, and her political theory "is of little interest" because it is marred by an "ill-thought out and unsystematic" effort to reconcile her hostility to the state with her rejection of anarchism.[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukathas199855-195) *[The Journal of Ayn Rand Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Journal_of_Ayn_Rand_Studies "The Journal of Ayn Rand Studies")*, a [peer-reviewed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer-reviewed "Peer-reviewed"), multidisciplinary [academic journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_journal "Academic journal") devoted to the study of Rand and her ideas, was established in 1999.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2012184-254)

### 21st-century academic reaction

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: 21st-century academic reaction")]

In 2009, historian Jennifer Burns identified "an explosion of scholarship" about Rand since 2000,[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009295%E2%80%93296-255) although as of that year, few universities included Rand or Objectivism as a philosophical specialty or research area.[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010116-256) From 2002 to 2012, over 60 colleges and universities accepted grants from the charitable foundation of [BB\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BB%26T "BB\&T") that required teaching Rand's ideas or works; in some cases, the grants were controversial or even rejected because of the requirement to teach about Rand.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlaherty2015-257)[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010116%E2%80%93117-258)

In a 2010 essay for the [Cato Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cato_Institute "Cato Institute"), Huemer argued very few people find Rand's ideas convincing, especially her ethics. He attributed the attention she receives to her being a "compelling writer", especially as a novelist.[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHuemer2010-259) In 2012, the [Pennsylvania State University Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_State_University_Press "Pennsylvania State University Press") agreed to take over publication of *The Journal of Ayn Rand Studies*,[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2012183-260) and the [University of Pittsburgh Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Pittsburgh_Press "University of Pittsburgh Press") launched an "Ayn Rand Society Philosophical Studies" series based on the Society's proceedings.[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeddon201475-261) The Fall 2012 update to the entry about Rand in the *[Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_Encyclopedia_of_Philosophy "Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy")* said that "only a few professional philosophers have taken her work seriously".[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBadhwarLong2020-6) That same year, political scientist [Alan Wolfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Wolfe "Alan Wolfe") dismissed Rand as a "nonperson" among academics, an attitude that writer Ben Murnane later described as "the traditional academic view" of Rand.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurnane20183-262) In a 2018 article for *[Aeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeon_\(magazine\) "Aeon (magazine)")*, philosopher Skye C. Cleary wrote: "Philosophers love to hate Ayn Rand. It's trendy to scoff at any mention of her." However, Cleary said that because many people take Rand's ideas seriously, philosophers "need to treat the Ayn Rand phenomenon seriously" and provide refutations rather than ignoring her.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECleary2018-242)

Media critic [Eric Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Burns "Eric Burns") said in 2020 that "Rand is surely the most engaging philosopher of my lifetime",[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2020261-263) but "nobody in the academe pays any attention to her, neither as an author nor a philosopher".[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2020259-264) That same year, the editor of a collection of critical essays about Rand said academics who disapproved of her ideas had long held "a stubborn resolve to ignore or ridicule" her work but that more were engaging with her work in recent years.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECocks202011,_15-265) In 2023, *The Journal of Ayn Rand Studies* ceased publication.[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2023-266)

## Legacy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Legacy")]

### Popular interest

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Popular interest")]

[![Dust jacket from Atlas Shrugged depicting railroad tracks](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Atlas_Shrugged_%281957_1st_ed%29_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg/170px-Atlas_Shrugged_%281957_1st_ed%29_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Atlas_Shrugged_\(1957_1st_ed\)_-_Ayn_Rand.jpg)

*[Atlas Shrugged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_Shrugged "Atlas Shrugged")* has sold more than 10 million copies.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOfford202212-267)

With over 37 million copies sold as of 2020[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit), Rand's books continue to be read widely.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOfford202212-267)[\[m\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-269) A survey conducted for the [Library of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Library_of_Congress "Library of Congress") and the [Book of the Month Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_the_Month_Club "Book of the Month Club") in 1991 asked club members to name the most influential book in their lives. Rand's *Atlas Shrugged* was the second most popular choice, after the Bible.[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty200711-270) Although Rand's influence has been greatest in the United States, there has been international interest in her work.[\[n\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-274)

Rand's contemporary admirers included fellow novelists, like [Ira Levin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ira_Levin "Ira Levin"), [Kay Nolte Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay_Nolte_Smith "Kay Nolte Smith") and [L. Neil Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Neil_Smith "L. Neil Smith"); she has influenced later writers like [Erika Holzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erika_Holzer "Erika Holzer"), [Terry Goodkind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Goodkind "Terry Goodkind"),[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERiggenbach200491%E2%80%93144-275) and comic book artist [Steve Ditko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Ditko "Steve Ditko").[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra20048%E2%80%9311-276) Rand provided a positive view of business and subsequently many business executives and entrepreneurs have admired and promoted her work.[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009168%E2%80%93171-277) Businessmen such as [John Allison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_A._Allison_IV "John A. Allison IV") of [BB\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BB%26T "BB\&T") and [Ed Snider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Snider "Ed Snider") of [Comcast Spectacor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast_Spectacor "Comcast Spectacor") have funded the promotion of Rand's ideas.[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009298-278)[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009412-279)

Television shows, movies, songs, and video games have referred to Rand and her works.[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra20044%E2%80%935-280)[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009282-281) Throughout her life she was the subject of many articles in popular magazines,[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999110%E2%80%93111-282) as well as book-length critiques by authors such as the psychologist Albert Ellis[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199998-283) and Trinity Foundation president John W. Robbins.[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999101-250) Rand or characters based on her figure prominently in novels by American authors,[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra20043-284) including Kay Nolte Smith, [Mary Gaitskill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Gaitskill "Mary Gaitskill"), [Matt Ruff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matt_Ruff "Matt Ruff"), and [Tobias Wolff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobias_Wolff "Tobias Wolff").[\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler202115%E2%80%9322-285) [Nick Gillespie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Gillespie "Nick Gillespie"), former editor-in-chief of *[Reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason_\(magazine\) "Reason (magazine)")*, remarked: "Rand's is a tortured immortality, one in which she's as likely to be a punch line as a protagonist. Jibes at Rand as cold and inhuman run through the popular culture."[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChadwickGillespie2005at_1:55-286) Two movies have been made about Rand's life. A 1997 documentary film, *[Ayn Rand: A Sense of Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayn_Rand:_A_Sense_of_Life "Ayn Rand: A Sense of Life")*, was nominated for the [Academy Award for Best Documentary Feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Documentary_Feature "Academy Award for Best Documentary Feature").[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999128-287) *[The Passion of Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Passion_of_Ayn_Rand_\(film\) "The Passion of Ayn Rand (film)")*, a 1999 television adaptation of the [book of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Passion_of_Ayn_Rand "The Passion of Ayn Rand"), won several awards.[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010122-288) Rand's image also appears on a [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_on_stamps_of_the_United_States#R "List of people on stamps of the United States") [U.S. postage stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postage_stamps_and_postal_history_of_the_United_States "Postage stamps and postal history of the United States") illustrated by artist [Nick Gaetano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Gaetano "Nick Gaetano").[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWozniak2001380-289)

Rand's works, most commonly *Anthem* or *The Fountainhead*, are sometimes assigned as secondary school reading.[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-290) Since 2002, the Ayn Rand Institute has provided free copies of Rand's novels to teachers who promise to include the books in their curriculum.[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuffy2012-291) The Institute had distributed 4.5 million copies in the U.S. and Canada by the end of 2020.[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ARI2020-268) In 2017, Rand was added to the required reading list for the [A Level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GCE_Advanced_Level_\(United_Kingdom\) "GCE Advanced Level (United Kingdom)") Politics exam in the United Kingdom.[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWang2017-292)

See also: [Objectivism and libertarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivism_and_libertarianism "Objectivism and libertarianism")

Although she rejected the labels "conservative" and "libertarian",[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009258-293)[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiss201255-294) Rand has had a continuing influence on [right-wing politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_politics "Right-wing politics") and libertarianism.[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns20094-295)[\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010107%E2%80%93108,_124-296) Rand is often considered one of the three most important women (along with [Rose Wilder Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Wilder_Lane "Rose Wilder Lane") and Isabel Paterson) in the early development of modern [American libertarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_libertarianism "American libertarianism").[\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2015746-297)[\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler202188-298) [David Nolan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Nolan_\(libertarian\) "David Nolan (libertarian)"), one founder of the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\) "Libertarian Party (United States)"), said that "without Ayn Rand, the libertarian movement would not exist".[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranden1986414-299) In his history of that movement, journalist [Brian Doherty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Doherty_\(journalist\) "Brian Doherty (journalist)") described her as "the most influential libertarian of the twentieth century to the public at large".[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty200711-270) Political scientist [Andrew Koppelman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Koppelman "Andrew Koppelman") called her "the most widely read libertarian".[\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoppelman202217-300) Historian Jennifer Burns referred to her as "the ultimate gateway drug to life on the right".[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns20094-295)

The political figures who cite Rand as an influence are usually conservatives (often members of the Republican Party),[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty200954-301) despite Rand taking some atypical positions for a conservative, like being [pro-choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-choice "Pro-choice") and an atheist.[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiss2012155-302) She faced intense opposition from [William F. Buckley Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_F._Buckley_Jr. "William F. Buckley Jr.") and other contributors to the conservative *National Review* magazine, which published numerous criticisms of her writings and ideas.[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2004139,_243-303) Nevertheless, a 1987 article in *The New York Times* called her the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_administration "Reagan administration")'s "novelist laureate".[\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009279-304) Republican [congressmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress "United States Congress") and conservative pundits have acknowledged her influence on their lives and have recommended her novels.[\[291\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009xii-305)[\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler2021184-306)[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009283-307) She has influenced some conservative politicians outside the U.S., such as [Sajid Javid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sajid_Javid "Sajid Javid") in the United Kingdom, [Siv Jensen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siv_Jensen "Siv Jensen") in Norway, and [Ayelet Shaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayelet_Shaked "Ayelet Shaked") in Israel.[\[294\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler2021174%E2%80%93184-308)[\[295\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERudoren2015-309)

[![Man holding a poster that says "I am John Galt"](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/TDTP08.JPG/220px-TDTP08.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TDTP08.JPG)

A protester's sign at a 2009 Tea Party rally refers to John Galt, the hero of Rand's *Atlas Shrugged*.

The [2007–2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%932008_financial_crisis "2007–2008 financial crisis") renewed interest in her works, especially *Atlas Shrugged*, which some saw as foreshadowing the crisis.[\[296\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009283%E2%80%93284-310)[\[297\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty200951%E2%80%9352-311) Opinion articles compared real-world events with the novel's plot.[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoherty200954-301)[\[298\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010125-312) Signs mentioning Rand and her fictional hero John Galt appeared at [Tea Party protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_protests "Tea Party protests").[\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2019xiv-313)[\[300\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler2021146-314) There was increased criticism of her ideas, especially from the [political left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_left "Political left"). Critics blamed the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession "Great Recession") on her support of [selfishness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rational_egoism#Ayn_Rand "Rational egoism") and [free markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_markets "Free markets"), particularly through her influence on Alan Greenspan.[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009283-307) In 2015, Adam Weiner said that through Greenspan, "Rand had effectively chucked a ticking time bomb into the boiler room of the US economy".[\[301\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeiner20202-315) Lisa Duggan said that Rand's novels had "incalculable impact" in encouraging the spread of [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal "Neoliberal") political ideas.[\[302\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuggan2019xiii-316) In 2021, [Cass Sunstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cass_Sunstein "Cass Sunstein") said Rand's ideas could be seen in the tax and regulatory policies of the [Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidency_of_Donald_Trump "First presidency of Donald Trump"), which he attributed to the "enduring influence" of Rand's fiction.[\[303\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESunstein2021145%E2%80%93146-317)

### Objectivist movement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Objectivist movement")]

[![Photo of Leonard Peikoff](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/55/Leonard_Peikoff_%28cropped%29.tiff/lossy-page1-170px-Leonard_Peikoff_%28cropped%29.tiff.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Leonard_Peikoff_\(cropped\).tiff)

Rand's heir [Leonard Peikoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_Peikoff "Leonard Peikoff") co-founded the Ayn Rand Institute.

Main article: [Objectivist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objectivist_movement "Objectivist movement")

After the closure of the Nathaniel Branden Institute, the Objectivist movement continued in other forms. In the 1970s, Peikoff began delivering courses on Objectivism.[\[304\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009249-318) In 1979, [Peter Schwartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Schwartz_\(writer\) "Peter Schwartz (writer)") started a newsletter called *[The Intellectual Activist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intellectual_Activist "The Intellectual Activist")*, which Rand endorsed.[\[305\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra2013402_n5-319)[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009276-124) She also endorsed *[The Objectivist Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Objectivist_Forum "The Objectivist Forum")*, a bimonthly magazine founded by Objectivist philosopher [Harry Binswanger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Binswanger "Harry Binswanger"), which ran from 1980 to 1987.[\[306\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein199979-320)

In 1985, Peikoff worked with businessman Ed Snider to establish the [Ayn Rand Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayn_Rand_Institute "Ayn Rand Institute"), a nonprofit organization dedicated to promoting Rand's ideas and works. In 1990, after an ideological disagreement with Peikoff, David Kelley founded the Institute for Objectivist Studies, now known as [The Atlas Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlas_Society "The Atlas Society").[\[307\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009280%E2%80%93281-321)[\[308\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein201019,_114-322) In 2001, historian John P. McCaskey organized the Anthem Foundation for Objectivist Scholarship, which provides grants for scholarly work on Objectivism in academia.[\[309\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2010117-323)

## Selected works

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Selected works")]

Main article: [Bibliography of Ayn Rand and Objectivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibliography_of_Ayn_Rand_and_Objectivism "Bibliography of Ayn Rand and Objectivism")

**Fiction and drama**

- *[Night of January 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_January_16th "Night of January 16th")* (performed 1934, published 1968)

- *[We the Living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_the_Living "We the Living")* (1936, revised 1959)

- *[Anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthem_\(novella\) "Anthem (novella)")* (1938, revised 1946)

- *[The Unconquered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Unconquered_\(1940_play\) "The Unconquered (1940 play)")* (performed 1940, published 2014)

- *[The Fountainhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fountainhead "The Fountainhead")* (1943)

- *[Atlas Shrugged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlas_Shrugged "Atlas Shrugged")* (1957)

- *[The Early Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Early_Ayn_Rand "The Early Ayn Rand")* (1984)

  - *[Ideal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideal_\(play\) "Ideal (play)")* (1936, performed 1989)
  - *Think Twice* (1939)

- *[Ideal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideal_\(novel\) "Ideal (novel)")* (based on the eponymous play, 2015)

**Non-fiction**

- *Pola Negri* (1925)
- *[For the New Intellectual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_the_New_Intellectual "For the New Intellectual")* (1961)
- *[The Virtue of Selfishness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Virtue_of_Selfishness "The Virtue of Selfishness")* (1964)
- *[Capitalism: The Unknown Ideal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism:_The_Unknown_Ideal "Capitalism: The Unknown Ideal")* (1966, expanded 1967)
- *[The Romantic Manifesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Romantic_Manifesto "The Romantic Manifesto")* (1969, expanded 1975)
- *[The New Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Left:_The_Anti-Industrial_Revolution "The New Left: The Anti-Industrial Revolution")* (1971, expanded 1975)
- *[Introduction to Objectivist Epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduction_to_Objectivist_Epistemology "Introduction to Objectivist Epistemology")* (1979, expanded 1990)
- *[Philosophy: Who Needs It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy:_Who_Needs_It "Philosophy: Who Needs It")* (1982)
- *[Letters of Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_of_Ayn_Rand "Letters of Ayn Rand")* (1995)
- *[Journals of Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journals_of_Ayn_Rand "Journals of Ayn Rand")* (1997)

## Notes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Ayn_Rand\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Notes")]

1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-1)** Rand's initial citizenship was in the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire "Russian Empire") and continued through the [Russian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Republic "Russian Republic") and the [Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic "Russian Soviet Federative Socialist Republic"), which became part of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union").
2. ^ [***a***](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-frank_3-0) [***b***](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-frank_3-1) Rand's husband, Charles Francis O'Connor (1897–1979),[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200965-2) is not to be confused with the actor and director [Frank O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_O%27Connor_\(director\) "Frank O'Connor (director)") (1881–1959) or the writer whose pen name was [Frank O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_O%27Connor "Frank O'Connor").
3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-5)** Russian: Алиса Зиновьевна Розенбаум, [\[ɐˈlʲisə zʲɪˈnovʲjɪvnə rəzʲɪnˈbaʊm\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Russian "Help:IPA/Russian"). Most sources [transliterate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transliterate "Transliterate") her given name as either *Alisa* or *Alissa*.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein1999121-4)
4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-15)** The city was renamed *Petrograd* from the Germanic *Saint Petersburg* in 1914 because Russia was at war with Germany. In 1924 it was renamed *Leningrad*. The name *Saint Petersburg* was restored in 1991.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIoffe2022-14)
5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-30)** She may have taken *Rand* as her surname because it is graphically similar to a vowelless excerpt Рзнб of her birth surname Розенбаум in [Cyrillic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrillic "Cyrillic").[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein20107-25)[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200955-26) Rand said *Ayn* was adapted from a [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language "Finnish language") name.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200919,_301-27) Some biographical sources question this, suggesting it may come from a nickname based on the Hebrew word עין (*[ayin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayin "Ayin")*, meaning 'eye').[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200955%E2%80%9357-28) Letters from Rand's family do not use such a nickname.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29)
6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-40)** Rand's immigration papers [anglicized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicisation_of_names "Anglicisation of names") her given name as *Alice*;[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200953-32) her legal married name became *Alice O'Connor*, but she did not use that name publicly or with friends.[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38)[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBranden198672-39)
7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-46)** It was later published in *[The Early Ayn Rand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Early_Ayn_Rand "The Early Ayn Rand")* along with other screenplays, plays, and short stories that were not produced or published during her lifetime.[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns200922-45)
8. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-50)** In 1941, [Paramount Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_Pictures "Paramount Pictures") produced a [movie loosely based on the play](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Night_of_January_16th_\(film\) "The Night of January 16th (film)"). Rand did not participate in the production and was highly critical of the result.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller200978-48)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein201087-49)
9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-57)** In 1942, the novel was adapted without permission into a pair of Italian films, *Noi vivi* and *Addio, Kira*. After Rand's post-war legal claims over the piracy were settled, the films were re-edited with her approval and released as *[We the Living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_the_Living_\(film\) "We the Living (film)")* in 1986.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56)
10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-71)** Their friendship ended in 1948 after Paterson made what Rand considered rude comments to valued political allies.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009130%E2%80%93131-69)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009214%E2%80%93215-70)
11. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-167)** Although she was previously friendly with *National Review* editor [William F. Buckley Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_F._Buckley_Jr. "William F. Buckley Jr."), Rand cut off all contact with him after the review was published.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeller2009285%E2%80%93286-165) Historian Jennifer Burns describes the review as a break between Buckley's religious conservatism and non-religious libertarianism.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009175-166)
12. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-176)** These include Twayne's United States Authors (*Ayn Rand* by James T. Baker), Twayne's Masterwork Studies (*The Fountainhead: An American Novel* by Den Uyl and *Atlas Shrugged: Manifesto of the Mind* by Gladstein), and Re-reading the Canon (*Feminist Interpretations of Ayn Rand*, edited by Gladstein and Sciabarra).[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESciabarra200343-175)
13. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-269)** This total includes 4.5 million copies purchased for free distribution to schools by the [Ayn Rand Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayn_Rand_Institute "Ayn Rand Institute") (ARI).[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ARI2020-268)
14. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-274)** Countries mentioned by sources discussing such interest include Australia, Denmark, France, Germany, Iceland, India, Israel, Monaco, the Netherlands, Spain, Sweden, Switzerland, and the United Kingdom.[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGladstein2003384%E2%80%93386-271)[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurnane20182%E2%80%933-272)[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBr%C3%BChwiler2021126,_129,_137%E2%80%93138,_145%E2%80%93157-273)
